    On the other hand, sing(f −1 ) = {0}, and 0 is a finite asymptotic value. So by
Theorem 7.48, the set I(f ) is not a spider’s web, for any a ∈ C. For a > 1/e, it can
also be deduced directly from the work of Devaney [105] that the set C \ I(f ) contains
an unbounded connected subset; in particular, I(f ) is not a spider’s web. See also a
paper by Osborne and Sixsmith [207, Example 2], where it is even shown that the set of
points with unbounded but non-escaping orbits has uncountably many pairwise disjoint
unbounded connected subsets.
   Jarque [151] and Rempe [223] obtained some generalisations of Theorem 7.52. In
particular, the following holds; see [151, Theorem A] and [223, Theorem 1.2, (a)].
Theorem 7.53. Let f (z) = aez , where a ∈ C \ {0}. If 0 ∈ I(f ) or if 0 is preperiodic,
then I(f ) is connected.
   The result in [223, Theorem 1.2] in fact applies to a much larger set of parameters a,
but the condition is somewhat technical to state. The article also asks the following
question [223, p. 73].
Question 7.54 (Rempe, 2011). Let a ∈ C be such that all periodic cycles of the
exponential map f (z) = aez are repelling. Is I(f ) connected?
   As we have seen, it may happen that I(f ) is connected and C \ I(f ) contains an
unbounded connected set. Rippon and Stallard [250, Question 10.2] raised the following
question.
Question 7.55 (Rippon and Stallard, 2019). Does there exist a transcendental entire
function f such that I(f ) is connected and C \ I(f ) contains an unbounded closed
connected set?
    It can be deduced from [223, Proposition 3.2] that such sets do not exist for the
exponential maps in Theorem 7.53, and in particular for f = exp. (For this function the
question was raised in [250, p. 3108].)
    What can be said about the structure of the escaping set, and its connected com-
ponents, when I(f ) is not connected? In §2.1 (and §2.3, in the case where fa is of
disjoint type) we encountered examples where the escaping set has infinitely many and
in fact uncountably many connected components. This extends to all disjoint-type entire
functions.
Proposition 7.56. Let f be an entire function of disjoint type and let R be an admissible
radius for f . Then J(f ) has uncountably many connected components that intersect
AR (f ).
   In particular, J(f ), I(f ), A(f ) and AR (f ) all have uncountably many connected
components.

                                           85
Proof. The claim follows from well-known properties of disjoint-type entire functions
(compare the discussion before Theorem 1.2 of [228]). The fact that J(f ) and I(f ) have
uncountably many connected components was noted in Theorem 3.10 and Corollary 3.11
of [224]. However, a proof for A(f ) and AR (f ) does not seem to appear in the literature,
so we sketch the argument here.
    Since f is of disjoint type, we have f ∈ B. Recall that I(f ) ⊂ J(f ) by Theorem 6.1.
Therefore the first claim in the proposition does indeed imply the final sentence.
    Let R̃ > R be sufficiently large (see below). Choose some z0 ∈ AR̃ (f ) ̸= ∅, and let
C0 be the connected component of J(f ) containing z0 . It follows from Proposition 8.1
and Corollary 8.2 of [224] that there exist ρ > 1 and M > 0 (depending only on f )
and an uncountable collection C of different connected components C of J(f ) with the
following property. For each C ∈ C there is a homeomorphism

                                          θC : C0 → C

with
                             |f n (z)|1/ρ ≤ |f n (θC (z)) ≤ |f n (z)|ρ
whenever |f n (z)| ≥ M . Assuming that R̃ was chosen sufficiently large, we have

                 |f n (θC (z0 ))| ≥ M n (R̃, f )1/ρ ≥ M n (R̃1/ρ , f ) ≥ M (R, f )

by Lemma 5.4, for all C ∈ C and all n ≥ 0. Hence each C ∈ C intersects M (R, f ), and
the proof is complete.
    For general entire functions we have the following theorem, which is a consequence
of a result of Rippon and Stallard [242, Theorem 5.2].

Theorem 7.57. Let f be a transcendental entire function, and let E ⊂ I(f ) be com-
pletely invariant under f such that J(f ) = E ∩ J(f ). Then either E is connected or it
has infinitely many connected components.

   Since the hypothesis holds for E = I(f ) and E = A(f ), either set is either connected
or has infinitely many connected components. The question whether “infinitely many”
can always be replaced by “uncountably many” remains open [250, Question 10.1].

Question 7.58 (Rippon and Stallard, 2019). Let f be a transcendental entire function.
For each of the sets I(f ) and A(f ), is it the case that it is either connected or it has
uncountably many connected components?

   The following result by Rippon and Stallard [250, Theorem 1.2] gives a partial result
towards Question 7.58 for I(f ).

Theorem 7.59. Let f be a transcendental entire function such that I(f ) is discon-
nected. If D is a disc intersecting J(f ), then I(f ) \ D has uncountably many connected
components that meet ∂D.


                                                86
   Concerning unbounded subsets of the levels AR (f ), Rippon and Stallard [250, The-
orem 1.3] have shown the following.
Theorem 7.60. Let f be a transcendental entire function and let R0 be an admissible
radius for f . Suppose there exists R ≥ R0 such that AR (f ) is not a spider’s web. Then
there is a dense subset of R ∈ [R0 , ∞) for which AR (f ) contains uncountably many
pairwise disjoint unbounded connected Fσ sets.
   In [250, Question 10.3], the authors ask a number of questions related to this theorem.
Question 7.61 (Rippon and Stallard, 2019). In Theorem 7.60, can we replace the
“unbounded connected Fσ sets” in AR (f ) by “unbounded connected closed sets” in
AR (f ) or even by “connected components” of AR (f )?
Question 7.62 (Rippon and Stallard, 2019). In Theorem 7.60, can we replace the
“dense set” of values of R ∈ [R0 , ∞) by “all” R ∈ [R0 , ∞)?
Question 7.63 (Rippon and Stallard, 2019). Does there exist a transcendental entire
function f and an admissible radius R for f such that AR (f ) is connected, but AR (f )
is not a spider’s web?
    We recall that, if f ∈ B, then AR (f ) is not a spider’s web by Corollary 7.34. It
can be shown in this case that AR (f ) does indeed have uncountably many connected
components, and so the answers to Questions 7.61 to 7.63 are positive. This can be
shown similarly as in Proposition 7.56, by adapting [224, Theorem 8.1] to the case of
general functions in the Eremenko–Lyubich class. Instead, we will deduce this fact
directly from Proposition 7.56 in Corollary 8.10 below.
    Recall from Theorem 7.53 that the escaping set of an exponential map f (z) = aez
is connected when 0 is preperiodic. Of course, the reason that the orbit of 0 plays an
important role is because sing(f −1 ) = {0}. It is interesting to note that the fact that 0
is an asymptotic value is crucial here. Indeed, Theorem 8.27 below (from [5]) describes
a large class of functions in the Eremenko–Lyubich class B (namely the class of strongly
geometrically finite functions with no asymptotic values in the Julia set and disconnected
escaping sets such that the answer to Question 7.58 is positive. This includes all cosine
maps f (z) = aez + be−z where a, b ∈ C \ {0} and both critical values of f are strictly
preperiodic, providing a counterpart to the second case in Theorem 7.53.

7.6    Counterexamples to Eremenko’s conjecture
As we have seen, a number of weaker statements than Eremenko’s conjecture hold for all
transcendental entire functions, while a number of stronger conjectures were shown to fail
some time ago. We have also discussed classes of entire functions for which Eremenko’s
conjecture is known to hold. Very recently Martı́-Pete, Rempe and Waterman [181]
showed that Eremenko’s conjecture is false in general.
Theorem 7.64. There exists a transcendental entire function f such that {0} is a
connected component of I(f ).

                                            87
    The construction is closely related to the ideas used to prove Theorem 6.46. Recall
that this theorem shows that certain bounded simply connected domains can be realised
as escaping wandering domains with non-escaping points on the boundary. As we already
mentioned, the same method of proof also produces oscillating wandering domains with
escaping points on the boundary. A key observation for Theorem 7.64 is that, with extra
care, the same ideas can also be applied to certain simple unbounded domains. Hence it
is possible to construct a transcendental entire function such that
 (a) The half-strip U ..= {a + ib : a > 0 and |b| < 1} is an oscillating wandering domain
     of f , and
 (b) 0 ∈ I(f ).
The construction ensures that there is a sequence of topological strips, each tending to
infinity in both directions and surrounding U and approximating ∂U more and more
closely, each of which is a subset of an attracting basin of f . This not only means
that ∂U is a subset of the Julia set (a key fact for establishing (a)), but also that the
connected component of I(f ) containing 0 is contained in U . Recall that U ∩ I(f ) = ∅,
and hence by Theorem 6.45, ∂U ∩ I(f ) has zero harmonic measure as seen from U .
Thus U ∩ I(f ) = ∂U ∩ I(f ) is totally disconnected. We conclude that {0} is a connected
component of I(f ), as claimed. (See [181, Remark 7.4].)
    In fact, it is possible to apply the same construction to obtain an example for which
0 is not on the boundary, by replacing the closure U by a straight ray (which one might
consider a degenerate half-strip of height zero). More precisely, the following holds [181,
Theorem 7.1].
Theorem 7.65. There exists a transcendental entire function f with the following prop-
erties.
 (a) The interval [0, ∞) is a connected component of J(f ) ∪ I(f ).
 (b) 0 ∈ I(f ), but t ∈
                      / I(f ) for t > 0.
   This construction is modified in [181, Theorem 1.2] to also construct non-singleton
bounded connected components of I(f ).
Theorem 7.66. Let K ⊂ C be a compact set such that C \ K is connected. Then there
exists a transcendental entire function f such that K is a connected component of I(f ).
    As noted in [181, Proposition 7.6], a bounded connected component of I(f ) need
not be compact, but if K is a compact component of I(f ), then C \ K is necessarily
connected. We refer to [181] for further discussion of the results stated in this section,
and their proofs.
    Recall from Theorem 7.4 that Eremenko’s conjecture holds – even in its strong form –
when f belongs to the Eremenko–Lyubich class B and also has finite order. In contrast,
it is clear from the construction in [181] that the functions in the preceding theorems
have both infinite order of growth and do not belong to the class B [181, Remark 7.5].
It is natural to ask whether or not this is necessary [181, Question 1.15].

                                            88
Question 7.67 (Martı́-Pete, Rempe and Waterman, 2025). Does Eremenko’s conjecture
hold for all functions in B?
    If not, we may ask the same question for the smaller class S.
Question 7.68. Does Eremenko’s conjecture hold for all functions in S?
Question 7.69 (Martı́-Pete, Rempe and Waterman, 2025). Is Eremenko’s conjecture
true for functions of finite order?
   Note that this is weaker than Question 7.8 which asks whether such functions even
have the strong Eremenko property.


8     Rigidity of escaping dynamics
Suppose that f and g are polynomials of the same degree. Then, by Böttcher’s theorem
(see, e.g., Milnor’s book [192, Theorem 9.1]), f and g are conformally conjugate in a
neighbourhood of infinity; that is, there exists a function θ conformal in a neighbourhood
U of ∞ such that
                                     θ(g(z)) = f (θ(z))                               (8.1)
for z ∈ U . This is one of the most important foundational results in the study of
the dynamics of complex polynomials. It means that the behaviour near infinity is
determined only by the degree of the map, and often this universal structure can be
used to obtain a good understanding of the function also away from infinity.
    More precisely, suppose that f is a polynomial of degree d ≥ 2, and that the Julia
set J(f ) is connected (which is equivalent to requiring that the postsingular set P (f ) be
bounded [192, Theorem 9.5]). In this case the escaping set I(f ), which is the basin of
the superattracting fixed point at ∞ of f , is simply connected (considered as a subset
of the Riemann sphere). Let us choose g in (8.1) to be the monomial z 7→ z d . Then the
local conjugacy θ between g and f extends to a biholomorphic map between I(g) = C\D
and I(f ), and (8.1) holds whenever |z| > 1. If, moreover, J(f ) is locally connected (i.e.,
every point has arbitrarily small connected neighbourhoods), then by the Carathéodory–
Torhorst theorem [192, Theorem 17.14], the map θ extends continuously to ∂D, giving
rise to a surjection θ : ∂D → J(f ). In other words, the dynamics of f on its Julia set
can be completely described as a quotient of the dynamics of g on the unit circle, which
corresponds to the d-tupling of angles. For this reason, the local connectivity of the
Julia set is an extremely important property in polynomial dynamics. We refer to [192,
§18] for further discussion of this construction.
    In contrast, even within simple families of entire functions, different maps may have
drastically different escaping dynamics. For example, consider the functions

                             fa : C → C,    z 7→ z + a + e−z ,

with a ∈ C. For a = 1, this is Fatou’s function (2.3), which has a completely invariant
Baker domain, in which Re fan (z) → +∞ locally uniformly; the same is true whenever

                                            89
Re a > 0. But for other values of a the escaping set I(fa ) can have a very different
structure.
    Indeed, for a = 0 the map fa has infinitely many Baker domains; see the discussion
after Theorem 6.44. As explained at the beginning of §6.5, the function fa has wandering
domains if a = 2πi − 1 and infinitely many attracting basins if a = −1. In the latter
case, I(fa ) has empty interior.
    Even within the family of exponential maps (2.4), fa (z) = aez , we saw that the global
structure of the escaping set varies dramatically. For instance, if 0 < a ≤ 1/e, then the
set I(fa ) has uncountably many connected components (see §2.3), while Theorem 7.52
says that if a > 1/e, then I(fa ) is connected. If, however, we restrict fa to the set of
points whose orbits under iteration stay sufficiently large, the situation becomes quite
different: In Proposition 2.6 we observed that the structure of this set is essentially
independent of the parameter a. It was shown by Rempe [218, Theorem 1.1] that
there is indeed an analogue of Böttcher’s theorem that connects the dynamics of any
two exponential maps on sets of this form. In [221], he extended this to all natural
parameter spaces (in a sense to be defined) within the Eremenko–Lyubich class B. The
goal of this section is to discuss these results and their implications. In particular, we
discuss the notion of “docile functions” introduced by Alhamed, Rempe and Sixsmith [5]
as an analogue of the local connectivity of polynomial Julia sets.
    There have been a number of developments since the original publication of [221] that
allow us to simplify and clarify the presentation of the theory. Where the statements we
make cannot be found in the existing literature, we provide their proofs, using results and
techniques from the relevant literature. These arguments are provided for completeness,
and are not required for understanding the other parts of the section.

8.1    Basic properties of quasiconformal mappings
Throughout the section, we will use terminology from the theory of quasiconformal
mappings. However, for most of our discussion, except §8.6, detailed knowledge of the
theory of quasiconformal mappings is not required. Informally, quasiconformal mappings
are homeomorphisms that do not distort too much. The formal definition is that a
homeomorphism φ between domains in C is called quasiconformal if it is absolutely
continuous on almost all lines parallel to the coordinate axes and if there is a constant
k < 1 such that
                                         ∂φ
                                              ≤k                                    (8.2)
                                         ∂φ
almost everywhere. (Here ∂φ and ∂φ denote the Wirtinger derivatives; that is, ∂φ =
(∂φ/∂x − i∂φ/∂y)/2 and ∂φ = (∂φ/∂x + i∂φ/∂y)/2.) The condition (8.2) means that
φ preserves angles (almost everywhere) up to a constant factor; the quantity ∂φ/∂φ is
called the complex dilatation of φ. One may extend the definition to the case where φ
is a homeomorphism between domains on the Riemann sphere.
    As mentioned, we will not require detailed knowledge about quasiconformal map-
pings, and readers unfamiliar with the theory should be able to follow most of the


                                            90
discussion if they keep the following key properties in mind.

Proposition 8.1.

 (a) Compositions and inverses of quasiconformal mappings are quasiconformal.

 (b) Non-constant complex affine maps from C to C are quasiconformal.

 (c) A quasiconformal map ψ is Hölder continuous at every point of the Riemann
     sphere; in particular there is K > 1 such that

                                    |z|1/K ≤ |ψ(z)| ≤ |z|K

      for sufficiently large z.

   Here by a complex affine map we mean a map of the form z 7→ az + b with a, b ∈ C.
A real affine map can be written in the form z 7→ az + bz + c with a, b, c ∈ C. We note
that such a map is quasiconformal if |b| < |a|, with complex dilatation equal to b/a.
   For a detailed introduction to quasiconformal mappings (including a proof of the
properties stated in Proposition 8.1) we refer to the books by Ahlfors [3] and Lehto and
Virtanen [168].

8.2    Eventual quasiconformal equivalence
In order to formulate an analogue of Böttcher’s theorem for the behaviour near infinity
of transcendental entire functions, we must first decide what should take the place of
the degree of the polynomial. It turns out that the correct notion is that of eventual
quasiconformal equivalence, introduced in [221]. (In [221], the term “quasiconformal
equivalence near ∞ is used; the more concise term “eventual quasiconformal equivalence”
is from [231].)

Definition 8.2. Let f, g ∈ B. Then we say that f and g are eventually quasiconformally
                        ∞
equivalent, and write f ∼
                        qc
                           g, if there exist quasiconformal homeomorphisms φ, ψ : C → C
and R > 0 such that
                                     ψ(g(z)) = f (φ(z))                             (8.3)
for all z ∈ C with max{|ψ(g(z))|, |f (φ(z))|} ≥ R.

Remark 8.3. (a) The definition also makes sense when f and g are polynomials: these
    are eventually quasiconformally equivalent if and only if they have the same degree.
                                                                                 ∞
 (b) Clearly pre- and post-composition of g ∈ B by affine maps yields a map f ∼  qc
     g. In particular, any two maps in the exponential family (2.4) are eventually
     quasiconformally equivalent. The reader may wish to keep this example in mind
     in the following.




                                           91
 (c) Definition 8.2 is motivated by work of Eremenko and Lyubich [116, §3]. For g ∈ S,
     they consider the set Mg of entire functions f topologically equivalent to g, i.e. those
     for which there exist homeomorphisms φ and ψ such that (8.3) holds for all z ∈ C.
     Eremenko and Lyubich show that Mg is a finite-dimensional analytic manifold,
     which may be considered the natural “parameter space” of g. If f ∈ Mg , then
     the maps φ and ψ can be chosen to be quasiconformal, and hence any two maps
     in the same Eremenko–Lyubich parameter space are eventually quasiconformally
     equivalent.

 (d) Any map of the form f (z) = p(z)ez , where p is a polynomial, is eventually quasi-
     conformally equivalent to g(z) = ez [221, Observation 2.7], but if p is non-constant,
     the two maps are not (globally) topologically equivalent in the sense of (c), since
     f has critical points but g does not.
                   ∞
  Suppose that f qc∼ g. It is often useful to be more explicit about the domain
where (8.3) holds.

Definition 8.4. Let f ∈ B. We say that an unbounded domain Wf ⊂ C is an initial
domain for f if

 (a) Wf contains a punctured neighbourhood of ∞ and its boundary (in C) is a Jordan
     curve, and

 (b) dist(sing(f −1 ), Wf ) > 0.

    Let f, g ∈ B and let Wf , Wg ⊂ C be initial domains for f and g, respectively. Suppose
that there exist quasiconformal maps ψ : C → C and φ : C → C with Wf = ψ(Wg ) and
f −1 (Wf ) = φ(g −1 (Wg )), and such that ψ ◦ g = f ◦ φ on g −1 (Wg ). Then we say that the
restrictions f : f −1 (Wf ) → Wf and g : g −1 (Wg ) → Wg are quasiconformally equivalent
(via ψ and φ), and write (f, Wf ) ∼ qc
                                       (g, Wg ).
                      ∞
    Observe that f ∼  qc
                           g if and only if there exist initial domains Wf and Wg such
that (f, Wf ) ∼
              qc
                 (g, Wg ).  The following result shows that the “only if” direction can be
strengthened.
                                                       ∞
Proposition 8.5. Suppose that f, g ∈ B are such that f ∼
                                                       qc
                                                          g. Let Wf and Wg be initial
domains for f and g, and suppose that there exists a quasiconformal homeomorphism
ψ : C → C with ψ(Wg ) = Wf .
    Then there exists a quasiconformal map φ : C → C such that (f, Wf ) ∼
                                                                        qc
                                                                           (g, Wg ) via
ψ and φ.

Proof. This is a result of Rempe and Waterman from a forthcoming article concern-
ing the notion of eventual quasiconformal equivalence [231]. As that article is still in
preparation, we briefly outline the argument.
   First, suppose that we already know that (f, Wf ) ∼  qc
                                                           (g, Wg ) via quasiconformal
homeomorphisms ψe and e   φ, where ψe = ψ on ∂Wg . We wish to show that there exists
a quasiconformal homeomorphism φ such that also (f, Wf ) ∼    qc
                                                                 (g, Wg ) via ψ and φ.

                                            92
(This observation already appears in [221, §3].) By assumption (b), for any connected
component V of g −1 (Wg ), the restriction g|V : V → Wg is a universal covering. The same
is true for f and Wf . It follows that ψ lifts to a homeomorphism φ : V → φ(V ) with
ψ ◦g = f ◦φ. Since g : ∂V → ∂Wg is also a covering map, this lift φ extends continuously
to ∂V ; the lift may be chosen so that this extension agrees with φ    e on ∂V . This defines φ
     −1                               −1
on g (Wg ). We define φ = φ    e on g (C \ Wg ), and obtain a homeomorphism φ : C → C.
This map is quasiconformal by a classical glueing lemma due to Royden [77, Lemma 2].
    Next we prove the proposition in the case where f = g and Wf ⊂ Wg . Choose two
Jordan domains D0 and D1 , bounded by analytic curves, such that sing(f −1 ) ⊂ D0 and
D0 ⊂ D1 ⊂ C \ Wg . If ∂D1 is chosen sufficiently close to ∂Wg , then ψ(∂D1 ) is close
to ψ(∂Wg ) = ∂Wf ⊂ Wg . In particular, we can choose D1 such that ψ(∂D1 ) ⊂ Wg ,
and in particular D0 ⊂ ψ(D1 ). Hence ψ(D1 ) \ D0 is an annulus, whose outer boundary
is the quasicircle ψ(∂D1 ) and whose inner boundary is the analytic curve ∂D0 . Then
there is a quasiconformal map ψe such that ψe = id on D0 and ψe = ψ on C \ D1 .
(See [169].) Now apply our first observation to the pairs (f, C \ D0 ) = (g, C \ D0 ),
which are trivially quasiconformally equivalent via the identity. Since ψe = id on ∂D0 ,
we obtain a quasiconformal map φ that satisfies ψe ◦ g = f ◦ φ on C \ g −1 (D0 ), and hence
ψ ◦ g = ψe ◦ g = f ◦ φ on g −1 (Wg ).
    Finally, let f , g, Wf , Wg and ψ be as in the statement of the proposition. We may
assume without loss of generality that 0 ∈     / Wg . Let ψ0 and φ0 be the maps from the
definition of eventual quasiconformal equivalence and, for some R ≫ 1, set Ug ..= R · Wg
and Uf ..= ψ0 (Ug ). Define ψg (z) ..= R · z and ψf ..= ψ0 ◦ ψg ◦ ψ −1 ; then ψg (Wg ) = Ug and
ψf (Wf ) = Uf . If R > 0 was chosen sufficiently large, then Ug ⊂ Wg and Uf ⊂ Wf , so
we may twice apply the special case we just established and obtain φf and φg such that
ψf ◦ f = f ◦ φf on f −1 (Wf ) and ψg ◦ g = g ◦ φg on C \ g −1 (Wg ).
    By choice of ψ0 and φ0 , we have ψ0 ◦ g = f ◦ φ0 on g −1 (Ug ), provided that R was
chosen sufficiently large. Setting φ ..= φ−1f ◦ φ0 ◦ φg , the proof is complete.

    In the definition of quasiconformal eqivalence, it is not essential that the functions f
and g are globally defined. More precisely, suppose that W = Wf is an initial domain
of f ∈ B, and consider Ω ..= f −1 (W ). Then Ω has the following properties:
                                 S
  (1) Ω is a disjoint union Ω = j Ωj of unbounded simply connected domains Ωj ;

 (2) any sequence of distinct Ωj accumulates only at infinity;

 (3) ∂Ωj is connected for each j.

Let h : W → C \ D be biholomorphic. For every Ωj , we can define a branch τ : Ωj → H>0
of log h ◦ f . This defines τ : Ω → H>0 such that

 (4) τ : Ωj → H>0 is biholomorphic for each j;

 (5) if (zn ) is a sequence in Ω such that τ (zn ) → ∞, then zn → ∞.



                                              93
Conversely, let W be any domain that is the outside of a Jordan curve, and let Ω ̸= ∅
and τ : Ω → H>0 have properties (1) to (5). Following Bishop [81], we say that f ..=
h−1 ◦ exp ◦τ is a model function. (We remark that Bishop restricts to the case where
W = C \ D and h = id, which can always be achieved by restricting f and conjugating
with a complex affine map.)
    The notion of eventual quasiconformal equivalence extends verbatim to model func-
tions, and it is natural to ask when a model is realised by an entire function in B up to
such equivalence. Following partial results by Rottenfußer, Rückert, Rempe and Schlei-
cher [251, §7] and Rempe [232, Theorems 1.7 and 1.8], Bishop [81] showed that this is
always the case.

Theorem 8.6. Suppose that f is a model function. Then there exists g ∈ B such that
 ∞
f∼
 qc
    g.

   In contrast, Bishop [82, Theorem 1.4] showed that Theorem 8.6 does not hold when
the class B is replaced by the Speiser class S.
   The results discussed in §8.3–8.6 all have appropriate versions for model functions,
with the same proofs, although for simplicity’s sake we will state them only for entire
functions. By Theorem 8.6, the possible dynamics near infinity is the same for functions
in B as it is for general model functions. Hence, to construct an entire function f ∈ B
with specific dynamical behaviour near infinity, it suffices to construct a model with the
same properties. See Theorem 8.17 below.
   For a further discussion of eventual quasiconformal equivalence, we refer to the forth-
coming paper [231].

8.3    Rigidity of escaping dynamics
Given a function f ∈ B we are interested in studying those points whose orbit under f
remains sufficiently large. Therefore we introduce the following notation.

Definition 8.7. Let f ∈ B, and let ρ > 0. We define

                     J≥ρ (f ) ..= {z ∈ J(f ) : |f n (z)| ≥ ρ for all n ≥ 0}.          (8.4)

    The argument used to prove that I(f ) ⊂ J(f ) for f ∈ B (Theorem 6.1) shows that,
if ρ is sufficiently large and z ∈ C satisfies |f n (z)| ≥ ρ for all n ≥ 0, then z ∈ J(f ).
Thus the condition z ∈ J(f ) in (8.4) can be replaced by z ∈ C when ρ is sufficiently
large (which is the only case of interest to us here).
    Now the main theorem of [221] can be stated as follows.
                                          ∞
Theorem 8.8. Suppose that f, g ∈ B with f ∼
                                          qc
                                             g. Then there exist ρ > 0 and η > 0 and
a quasiconformal homeomorphism θ : C → C such that

                                      θ(g(z)) = f (θ(z))                              (8.5)

whenever z ∈ J≥ρ (g) ∪ θ−1 (J≥η (f )).

                                               94
   Theorem 8.8 may be considered an analogue of Böttcher’s theorem for functions
in B. Since every escaping point z ∈ I(g) eventually enters J≥ρ (g), for every ρ > 0,
and similarly for f , Theorem 8.8 suggests that there is a close relationship between the
                                       ∞
escaping dynamics of f and g when f ∼  qc
                                          g. In particular, we have the following.

Proposition 8.9. Let f , g, ρ and θ be as in Theorem 8.8, and let z ∈ J≥ρ (g). Then
θ(z) ∈ I(f ) if and only if z ∈ I(g), and θ(z) ∈ A(f ) if and only if z ∈ A(g).
   Moreover, if R ≥ ρ is an admissible radius for g, then there exists an admissible
radius S ≥ η for f such that AS (f ) ⊂ θ(AR (g)). Here AS (f ) and AR (g) are the levels
of A(f ) and A(g) defined in (5.12).

Proof. We have f n (θ(z)) = θ(g n (z)), so f n (θ(z)) → ∞ if and only if g n (z) → ∞. This
proves the first claim.
    We next prove the statement about the fast escaping sets. Let ψ and φ be as in
Definition 8.2. By Proposition 8.1(c), the maps φ and φ−1 are both Hölder continuous.
So there is K > 1 such that |z|1/K ≤ |φ(z)| ≤ |z|K for all sufficiently large z, and
similarly for ψ and θ. It follows that the orders of growth of f and g are related: When
r is large enough,
                         M (r, f ) ≥ M (r1/K , g)1/K = Hε (r1/K )K ,
where ε ..= 1/K 2 and Hε (r) = M (r, g)ε as defined in (5.21). Let R > 0 be large and put
S ..= RK . Let z ∈ C be such that θ(z) ∈ A(f ). Then there exists L ∈ N such that
                                                            K
                 |f n+L (θ(z))| ≥ M n (S, f ) ≥ Hεn S 1/K         = Hεn (R)K

and hence
               |g n+L (z)| = |θ−1 (f n+L (θ(z)))| ≥ |f n+L (θ(z))|1/K ≥ Hεn (R)
for all n ∈ N. Thus z ∈ Q(g), where Q(g) is the quite fast escaping set defined in (5.22).
By Theorem 5.13, and since g ∈ B, we also have z ∈ A(g). So z ∈ A(g) if θ(z) ∈ A(f ).
The converse implication follows by exchanging the roles of f and g.
    The result about the levels AR (g) and AS (f ) follows by a similar reasoning from the
proof of Theorem 5.13 by Rippon and Stallard [247] (more precisely from Theorem 1.3
and formula (1.3) in that paper). We omit the details.
    As mentioned in §7.5, Theorem 8.8 can be used to give a positive answer to Ques-
tions 7.61, 7.62 and 7.63 when f ∈ B.

Corollary 8.10. Let f ∈ B and let R be an admissible radius for f . Then AR (f ) has
uncountably many connected components.
               ∞
Proof. Let g ∼qc
                 f be of disjoint type. (For example, we may take g = λf , where |λ| is
sufficiently small.) Let θ be as in Theorem 8.8. Applying Proposition 8.9 twice (once
to f and g and once to g and f ), for any sufficiently large S > 0, there are R > R′ ≥ ρ
with θ(AR′ (g)) ⊃ AS (f ) ⊃ θ(AR (g)). (Here ρ is as in Theorem 8.8.)
    By Proposition 7.56, there are uncountably many connected components of J(g) that
intersect AR (g). It follows that AS (f ) also has uncountably many connected components.

                                             95
    This proves the claim of the corollary when R is sufficiently large. For an arbitrary
admissible radius R, let n be so large that AM n (R,f ) (f ) has uncountably many connected
components. But f n (AR (f )) = AM n (R,f ) (f ). Since f n is an open mapping and AR (f )
is closed, it follows that AR (f ) also has uncountably many connected components, as
claimed.
    In order to be able to discuss further properties of the map θ from Theorem 8.8, we
sketch a proof of the theorem. In [221], the theorem is proved by applying a logarithmic
change of variable to f and g, as discussed in §6.1.
    Here, we instead describe how to construct the function θ directly, without passing to
logarithmic coordinates, making use of Proposition 8.5. The underlying idea is similar
to the classical proof of Böttcher’s theorem: Given z ∈ J≥R (f ), iterate forward under g,
cross to the dynamical plane of f using the quasiconformal map ψ, and then iterate
backwards under f , taking care to use the “correct” branches of f −1 . As the number of
iterates taken grows, the value obtained converges to the desired value θ(z).
    To make this precise, we need to clarify how to identify inverse branches of f near
infinity with those of g. We do so using a well-established way of identifying different
branches of f −1 (and g −1 ) near infinity, using “fundamental domains”. (Compare [42,
§2].)
    Suppose that Wf is an initial domain for f , with the additional property that

                                     f −1 (Wf ) ̸⊃ C \ Wf ,                               (8.6)

which can be ensured for example by requiring that 0, f (0) ∈   / Wf . Since every component
       −1
of ∂f (Wf ) is an arc tending to infinity in both directions, it follows that there exists an
arc δ ⊂ Wf \f −1 (Wf ) that connects a point of ∂Wf to ∞. Indeed, if ∂f −1 (Wf )∩∂Wf ̸= ∅,
then we can take δ ⊂ Wf ∩ ∂f −1 (Wf ). Otherwise, ∂Wf ∩ f −1 (Wf ) = ∅ by (8.6), and
hence there is a curve connecting ∂Wf to some point of ∂f −1 (Wf ) within Wf \ f −1 (Wf ),
and the latter point can be connected to ∞ in ∂f −1 (Wf ).
     Set Wf0 ..= Wf \ δ. Recall that f : f −1 (Wf ) → Wf is a covering map. Since Wf0
is simply connected, every connected component F of f −1 (Wf0 ) is simply connected
and f : F → Wf0 is biholomorphic. These domains F are called fundamental domains
of f (defined by Wf and δ). If z ∈ C is such that f n (z) ∈ Wf for all n ≥ 0, then
f n (z) ∈ f −1 (Wf0 ) for all n ≥ 0. (This is why it is important to choose δ ⊂ f −1 (C \ Wf ).)
     We can carry out the same operation for g, obtaining an initial domain Wg , a curve
γ ⊂ Wg \ f −1 (Wg ) and corresponding fundamental domains of g. If (f, Wf ) ∼        qc
                                                                                        (g, Wg )
via ψ and φ, where ψ is such that ψ(γ) = δ, then the map φ induces a correspondence
between the fundamental domains of g and f , and thus between the branches of g −1 and
f −1 . The next proposition shows that this can always be arranged.
                           ∞
Proposition 8.11. Let f ∼  qc
                              g. Then there exist initial domains Wf , Wg and quasicon-
formal maps ψ, φ such that (f, Wf ) ∼qc
                                        (g, Wg ) via ψ and φ, and satisfying the following
additional property. There exists an arc γ ⊂ Wg \ g −1 (Wg ) connecting a point of ∂Wg to
∞ with ψ(γ) ∩ f −1 (Wf ) = ∅.


                                              96
Proof. By assumption, there are initial domains Uf and Ug such that (f, Uf ) and (g, Ug )
are quasiconformally equivalent via maps ψ0 and φ0 . Let γ0 be a piece of ∂g −1 (Ug )
connecting some finite point to ∞.
   Set ψ ..= φ0 ; then

                     ψ(γ0 ) = φ0 (γ0 ) ⊂ φ0 (C \ g −1 (Ug )) = C \ f −1 (Uf ).

Set Wg ..= C \ D(0, ρ), where ρ is sufficiently large to ensure that Wg ⊂ Ug , Wf ..=
ψ(Wg ) ⊂ Uf and γ0 ∩ ∂Wg ̸= ∅. By Proposition 8.5, there exists φ such that (f, Uf )
and (g, Ug ) are quasiconformally equivalent via ψ and φ. Let γ be the piece of γ0 that
connects ∂Wg to infinity in Wg ; then γ has the desired properties.
                                                         ∞
    Now we can sketch the proof of Theorem 8.8. Let f ∼  qc
                                                            g, and apply Proposition 8.11.
Define Wg = Wg \ γ and Wf = Wf \ ψ(γ); then the connected components of g −1 (Wg0 )
          0 ..              0 ..

and f −1 (Wf0 ) are fundamental domains of g and f , respectively. Since ψ(Wg0 ) = Wf0 ,
the map φ induces a bijection between the set of fundamental domains of g and the set
of fundamental domains of f .
    To construct the conjugacy θ, define θ0 ..= ψ, and inductively define

                                  θn+1 (z) = f |−1
                                                φ(Fz ) (θn (g(z)))                          (8.7)

where Fz is the fundamental domain of g containing z. If θn (z) is defined, then g k (z) ∈
Wg0 for k = 1, . . . , n. Given such z, let F0 , F1 , . . . , Fn−1 be the fundamental domains of g
containing z, g(z), . . . , g n−1 (z). Then θn (z) is the element of f −n (ψ(g n (z))) that passes
through the fundamental domains φ(F0 ), φ(F1 ), . . . , φ(Fn−1 ), if such a point exists.
    It can be shown that, if z ∈ J≥ρ (g), where ρ is sufficiently large, then θn (z) is defined
for all n ≥ 0. Moreover, the hyperbolic distance in Wf between θn (z) and θn+1 (z)
tends to zero exponentially fast, uniformly on J≥ρ (g). (The proof uses the expansion
properties of f and g, and fundamental properties of quasiconformal mappings; see [221,
Lemma 2.6] for details.) Hence the maps (θn ) form a Cauchy sequence and converge
uniformly to a continuous function θ on J≥ρ (g) satisfying (8.5).
    If z ∈ J≥ρ (g), then the point w ..= θ(z) has the property that f n (w) and φ(g n (z))
belong to the same fundamental domain of f , for all n ≥ 0, and the hyperbolic distance
between the two points (in f −1 (Wf )) is uniformly bounded. These two properties de-
termine the point w uniquely, and we see that θ is injective. When we exchange the
roles of f and g in our construction, we obtain a map on J≥η (f ) for sufficiently large η.
It is not difficult to see that this map agrees with θ−1 when η is sufficiently large, and
thus (8.5) also holds on θ−1 (J≥η (f )).
    This establishes the theorem except for the claim that θ extends to a quasiconformal
homeomorphism of C. The latter is deduced in an indirect fashion: the maps f and
g are embedded in a holomorphic family of (not necessarily globally defined) model
functions in the sense of §8.2. Fixing g, the function θ depends holomorphically on f
(see [221, Proposition 3.6]), and hence provides a holomorphic motion of the set J≥R (f ).
A theorem of Bers and Royden [78, Theorem 1] then implies that θ extends to a global
quasiconformal map, as claimed. We refer to [221] for details.

                                                97
    If θ is a map as constructed in the proof of Theorem 8.8, we shall say that θ is a
generalised Böttcher map for f and g. (That is, θ : C → C is quasiconformal, and for
some ρ > 0 the map θ|J≥ρ (g) is the limit of the sequence (θn ) defined via (8.7), for some
initial configuration as in Proposition 8.11.)
    We note that the above proof of the existence of a global generalised Böttcher map
is highly non-constructive and non-canonical. In particular, the map θ does not relate
the dynamics of g to that of f outside the nowhere dense set J≥ρ (g). It is an interesting
question whether the extension can also be constructed directly, and whether it can be
chosen to conjugate f and g on a larger set. (We will see in §8.5 below that the answer
is positive when both functions are of disjoint type.)
                                    ∞
Question 8.12. Let f, g ∈ B with f ∼qc
                                       g. Is it possible to construct a global generalised
Böttcher map θ : C → C explicitly, without using holomorphic motions or parameter
arguments?
                                         ∞
Question 8.13. Let f, g ∈ B with f ∼     qc
                                            g. Does there always exist a generalised
Böttcher map θ for f and g so that the conjugacy relation (8.5) holds also on an open
neighbourhood of J≥ρ (g)?

8.4    Non-uniqueness of the conjugacy
Let f and g be polynomials of degree d. The conformal conjugacy θ near ∞ between f
and g given by (8.1) is not unique when d ≥ 2, but depends on a choice of normalisation.
More precisely, if a and b are the leading coefficients of f and g, then θ(z) ∼ cz as z → ∞,
where cd−1 = b/a. The map for a given c is unique, but each (d − 1)-th root of b/a can
be realised. (Usually the books, e.g. [192, Theorem 9.1], treat only the case g(z) = z d ,
but the general case follows easily from this.)
   A similar phenomenon occurs with the generalised Böttcher maps constructed in the
previous subsection. Let us illustrate this with an example, where f (z) ..= sin(z) and
g(z) ..= cos(z). Set ψ ..= id and consider the maps φ1 (z) ..= z + π/2, φ2 (z) ..= z − 3π/2
and φ3 (z) ..= −z + π/2. Then ψ ◦ g = f ◦ φj for each j ∈ {1, 2, 3}. Let Wf ..= Wg ..=
g({z ∈ C : |Im z| > 1). Then ∂Wf is an analytic Jordan curve (in fact, the ellipse with
focal points 1 and −1 that passes through the point cosh(1)). Set γ ..= [cosh(1), ∞).
Then the preimage of Wg0 ..= Wg \ γ is

                              {a + ib : |b| > 1 and a ∈
                                                      / 2πZ}.

So the fundamental domains of g corresponding to Wg and γ are vertical half-strips in
the upper and lower half-planes. Similarly, the fundamental domains of f corresponding
to Wf and ψ(γ) = γ are the connected components of

                          {a + ib : |b| > 1 and a ∈
                                                  / π/2 + 2πZ}.

   Clearly each of the maps φj induces a different bijection between the fundamental
domains of f and g, and hence all three give rise to different generalised Böttcher maps.

                                            98
(Recall that, for large R, the function θ maps points of JR (f ) belonging to a fundamental
domain F to points in the fundamental domain φ(F ).)
   On the other hand, this is the only ambiguity that is possible for the values of a
generalised Böttcher map on J≥ρ (g). To make this statement more precise, we continue
the notation from the proof of Theorem 8.8. The following uniqueness statement is a
consequence of [221, Corollary 4.2].
                                   ∞
Theorem 8.14. Suppose that f ∼    qc
                                      g, that θ is a generalised Böttcher map for f and g,
and that ρ is as in Theorem 8.8. Then for every Q > ρ there is Q′ > 0 with the following
property.
    Suppose that θ̃ : J≥Q (g) → J(f ) is a conjugacy between f and g such that θ̃(z) → ∞
as z → ∞, and such that θ(z) and θ̃(z) belong to the same fundamental domain of f (as
defined in the proof of Theorem 8.8) for all z ∈ J≥Q (g). Then θ̃ = θ on J≥Q′ (g).

    In the case where θ and θ̃ both are generalised Böttcher maps, this leads to a unique-
ness statement that can be phrased as follows. A sequence s = s0 s1 s2 . . . of fundamental
domains sk of f is called an external address of f . A point z has external address s for
f if f k (z) ∈ sk for k ≥ 0. If σ > 0 is sufficiently large, then every point z ∈ J≥σ (f )
has an external address. (We previously discussed external addresses in connection with
Theorem 7.27. If f is postsingularly bounded, and if σ is sufficiently large, then two
points have the same address in the sense discussed there if and only if they visit the
same sequence of fundamental domains. See also §8.8.)
    We remark that the definition of external addresses depends on the initial configura-
tion given by Wf and the curve δ. However, this dependence is not essential: given two
different such configurations, there is a natural bijection between the two corresponding
sets of external addresses (see the discussion preceding [42, Observation 4.12]).
    Hence it makes sense to speak about external addresses of g and external addresses
of f . Any generalised Böttcher map θ induces a natural bijection between the addresses
of g and those of f . It is this bijection that essentially determines θ: If θ̃ is another
generalised Böttcher map that induces the same bijection, then θ and θ̃ agree on J≥ρ (g)
for sufficiently large ρ.

8.5    The case of disjoint-type maps
We now return to Theorem 8.8, in the special case where both f and g are of disjoint
type. It turns out that in this case, the answer to Questions 8.12 and 8.13 is positive;
that is, we are able to construct a generalised Böttcher map for f and g more directly.
Recall from Proposition 3.5 that f is of disjoint type if and only if Wf can be chosen
such that f −1 (Wf ) ⊂ Wf .

Theorem 8.15. Let f, g ∈ B be of disjoint type, and let Wf and Wg be initial domains
for f and g, with the additional property that f −1 (Wf ) ⊂ Wf and g −1 (Wg ) ⊂ Wg .
    Assume that (f, Wf ) ∼
                         qc
                             (g, Wg ) via quasiconformal maps ψ and φ with φ = ψ on
C \ Wg . Then there exists a quasiconformal homeomorphism θ : C → C such that


                                            99
 (a) f ◦ θ = θ ◦ g on g −1 (Wg ) and
 (b) θ = φ on C \ g −1 (Wg ).
   The map θ is unique and depends only on the values of φ on C \ g −1 (Wg ), but not
on the values of ψ on Wg . Moreover, θ is a generalised Böttcher map.
Proof. The existence of θ is proved using a pull-back argument. Using the fact that
f ◦ φ = φ ◦ g on ∂g −1 (Wg ), it is easy to construct a sequence (θn ) of quasiconformal
maps with θ0 = φ, θn ◦g = f ◦θn+1 on g −1 (Wg ), and such that θn = φ on C\g −1 (Wg ). All
the (θn ) have the same maximal dilation and the sequence (θn (z)) is eventually constant
for every z in the dense open set C \ J(g). It follows that θn converges to the desired
function θ; we refer to the proof of [221, Theorem 3.1] for details.
    The conditions (a) and (b) mean that the values of φ on C \ g −1 (Wg ) determine θ
uniquely on the open and dense set C \ J(g), and hence on all of C. This proves the
second claim.
    Finally, set ψ0 ..= φ and φ0 ..= θ1 , where θ1 is as defined in the construction of θ.
Then (f, Wf ) ∼ qc
                   (g, Wg ) via ψ0 and φ0 . Moreover, these maps satisfy the additional
property from Proposition 8.11, for any curve γ as in the statement of the proposition.
(Indeed, φ(Wg \ g −1 (Wg )) = Wf \ f −1 (Wf ) by assumption.) If we follow the proof of
Theorem 8.8 starting with this configuration, then the resulting sequence of maps θn
agrees with the sequence of quasiconformal maps constructed at the beginning of this
proof, which converge to θ. Thus θ is a generalised Böttcher map, as claimed.
                                                            ∞
Proposition 8.16. Let f and g be of disjoint type with f ∼  qc
                                                               g. Let θ̃ be any generalised
Böttcher map for f and g. Then there exists ρ ≥ 0 and a generalised Böttcher map θ
with θ = θ̃ on J≥ρ (g) and such that θ restricts to a conjugacy between g|J(g) and f |J(f ) .
    (In fact, θ has the properties stated in Theorem 8.15, for a suitable choice of Wf ,
Wg , ψ and φ.)
Proof. By Proposition 3.5, we may choose initial domains Wf and Wg with f −1 (Wf ) ⊂
Wf , and similarly for Wg . Moreover, ∂Wf and ∂Wg can be chosen to be analytic Jordan
domains.
    Now suppose that we are given a configuration as in Proposition 8.11; that is, Uf
and Ug are initial domains for f and g, we have (f, Uf ) ∼   qc

                 −1
and γ0 ⊂ Ug \ g (Ug ) is an arc connecting a point of ∂Ug to ∞, such that δ0 ..= ψ0 (γ0 ) ⊂

associated to this configuration.

not affect the values of θ̃ on J≥ρ (g) for sufficiently large ρ. Similarly, we assume that

of the plane). The same is true for ∆f , Yf , Df and hf defined analogously.
